# plan-v1 — lecture companion notes, lec01–lec12

## Summary
[Judgment] Make all 12 companions useful at a glance to the lecturer without sacrificing their reference value. Start every article with the slide's actual message in about two plain sentences; then explain how to read its exhibit; put supporting detail and source locators below. This is a notes-only editorial job, not a rewrite of the lectures. Frozen baseline `99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d`. Audience, arguments, titles, examples, core-path timing and optional deep material remain those of the existing decks. Approximately 902 articles, organized as P1–P12; all packages are authorized now, not a one-lecture pilot.

## Coverage
| Comment | Baseline pages / titles | Stable IDs | Package |
|---|---|---|---|
| C01 | Every page of lec01–lec12, mapped below | All IDs below | P1–P12 |
| C02 | Every article; content and figure entries receive the full two-layer treatment | All IDs below | P1–P12 |
| C03 | All 12 companions, package checkpoints, capacity hold and resumption | All IDs below | P1–P12 + owner recovery |

## Per-entry specification applied to EVERY row below
1. **Must [judgment] — message first.** The article opens with a clear short answer to “What does this slide show, and why is it here?” Normally two short sentences, about 30–50 words total including the takeaway if it is separately labelled. The point must be substantive, not “This slide discusses X,” a title repetition, or instructions to the lecturer. Preserve essential qualifiers in this opening. Do not force a false or opaque message to meet a mechanical count. Long speaking scripts move to the detail layer rather than disappear. A one-sentence structural entry is enough for a title/divider/closer.
2. **Must [judgment] — explain the exhibit.** For a plot: identify population/model/setup, x and y with units, curves/colours/panels, direction of better if meaningful, the actual comparison/trend, and what cannot be inferred. For a table: denominator, row/column comparison, then the result. For a diagram: objects, arrows, operation and outcome in the displayed order. For an equation: define symbols, intuition and assumptions; longer derivations remain optional. For an example/transcript: what was asked, what happened, what lesson follows, and whether it is illustrative or observed. Do not leave only “Figure N shows the result.” Put this concise reading explanation after the opening and before optional deep details; no need to force all axes into the two-sentence opening.
3. **Must — details second, and no evidence loss.** Keep useful definitions, setup/model/date, calculations/proofs, qualifications, historical context, minor points and references available below the short layer. Remove genuinely duplicated narration, not distinct evidence. Distinguish empirical result, hypothetical calculation, source authors' interpretation and teaching analogy. Preserve quotation attribution and whether a source is cropped, translated, redrawn or illustrative. Do not expand attack examples into actionable payloads that the baseline deliberately omits.
4. **Must — source traceability.** For each numerical/empirical claim and figure retain a directly relevant source link and exact figure/table/section/page when available. Reuse already verified source metadata; do not substitute a search page. If an article currently relies on a neighboring entry's source, add a local link or precise internal cross-reference, not “see above.” Research is targeted fact-checking only. Do not add facts from memory or claim newly verified sources merely because links exist. Preserve dated historical framing; verify any proposed current legal/product claim against its authoritative source. If the main slide itself appears wrong, flag it as a plan issue rather than quietly changing the slide or making its note contradict it.
5. **Must — mapping and isolation.** One article per main slide, in order, with matching stable ID and title (page/section prefixes and mathematically equivalent title notation are fine). Main slide count/order/content, figures and teaching time remain unchanged. Before note changes, add IDs to lec01–lec11 main slides and paired articles in a single ID-only commit, with no other change; use the S01… sequence specified below and report the exact commit. Preserve lec12 IDs, including lettered splits. IDs are scoped by deck: lec03/S07 is not lec04/S07. No technical-supplement source edits.
6. **Prefer — restrained structure.** Use the existing companion design and a consistent two-layer reading order, not a new design system or a rigid multi-heading template for trivial entries. Keep short prose, descriptive headings and local source locators. No word-count ceiling applies to the retained deep reference material. English remains English. Optional teaching cues belong after the substance; do not add activities, extend lecture timing or invent pacing.
7. **Slides decides [judgment].** Exact wording, local note headings, organization of supporting material, and whether a separately labelled takeaway is needed to avoid repeating the opening. Preserve usability and compatibility with existing validators; if the script requires Key takeaway, keep a short one without duplicative padding. No markup/CSS/font instructions are prescribed by this plan.
8. **Acceptance for EVERY row.** Read its matching slide/exhibit and the entire revised note. A lecturer understands the specific message from the opening and can explain the displayed exhibit from the following explanation; details substantiate rather than contradict it. Required source limits and migrated material survive. Title/ID/order match. Note content must be substantively reviewed even when the baseline already passes; unchanged entries need an explicit reason in the work report. Template insertion or mechanical relocation alone does not meet C02.

The per-row “point seed” is the existing takeaway, included to identify intent, not an approved factual assertion or mandatory final wording. Revise overbroad seeds to the qualified message supported by their slide and original source. Proposed main-slide title = the baseline title below; do not retitle the deck. All article rows inherit requirements 1–8 above. Layout: existing plain-HTML companion; main layout untouched. Time: preserve existing pacing, including earlier lectures' shorter lectures/core paths. This plan does not impose 90 minutes on every lecture.

## Work packages and dependencies
Each Pn owns exactly the canonical note file paired with lecn. Note edits can be executed independently; recommended order P1→P12 for continuity and durable checkpointing. Commit after each completed lecture, recording its package, note count, substantive checks and any unresolved source issues. Implementation delegation, if useful, remains slides' responsibility; the owner never writes deck/note sources. Pn may read other lectures and tech supplements but not edit them. Shared ID addition precedes all package changes. Shared OUTLINE updates follow package integration. No content is approved until the complete first-round work report names an exact commit.

Capacity: follow job.md and C03. A known five-hour limit on either provider means hold further work, preserve the last completed lecture/commit, and resume only after reset/availability is established. Do not bypass with another model/account. The owner has a gated recovery check for a reported hold or prolonged inactivity; inactivity alone is not a confirmed quota event. If tools are unavailable during a hard limit, use existing durable package commits on recovery. This is not a new review round and must not trigger a full rewrite of finished packages.

## Package-specific safeguards [judgment]
- **P1:** Do not turn examples into universal rules (e.g. a deployer always legally owns every bot statement); clearly separate reported incident, observed outcome and broad lesson. Keep historical dates and legal limitations.
- **P2:** Define the protected unit/adjacency, epsilon/delta and assumptions. Noise is not automatically DP; deterministic constant outputs are not excluded by DP. Qualify slogans such as “No randomness, no privacy.”
- **P3:** Keep membership, extraction and dataset inference distinct; state access needed to compute loss; define FPR/TPR denominators and base rates. Separate laboratory measurements from extrapolation (Homer) and model-specific LiRA outcomes.
- **P4:** Keep exact, near-verbatim and semantic copying distinct. Separate measured extraction/membership from copyright allegations, legal findings and settlements. Keep matched non-member control logic.
- **P5:** Preserve the four unlearning targets and evidence levels. Replace “Correct and useless” (p23 seed) with a concrete computational-cost tradeoff, not a universal uselessness claim. Behavioral suppression is not established knowledge removal.
- **P6:** Keep calibration, factual correctness and conformal set coverage distinct. Maintain exchangeability, finite-sample/tie scope and marginal vs conditional coverage; simplify without deleting these.
- **P7:** Keep observed behavior and stated reasoning separate from inferred motives; identify artificial conflict, trained vs prompted condition, external graders and access. Fold long incident/test narration into supporting detail; no dilution of the experiment's setup.
- **P8:** State the perturbation/access threat model; empirical attack success is not a certificate. Keep stability vs correctness distinct and the statistical confidence/assumptions for smoothing.
- **P9:** Preserve training-time vs inference-time distinctions, triggered vs clean performance, targeted mapping, and measured poisoning success vs merely estimated data-control cost.
- **P10:** Keep model/version, access, attempts/budget, denominator and judge attached to ASR claims. Clarify plot direction and distinguish comparable vs incomparable budget units. No additional functioning jailbreak instructions.
- **P11:** Distinguish embedded watermark, unkeyed detection and signed provenance. Preserve fresh-key vs reused-key scope, theoretical/nominal vs measured FPR, and origin vs truth. p27's long cryptographic explanation needs a plain-language opening, not deletion of assumptions.
- **P12:** Preserve the newly merged 80-slide version. Explain each rate's denominator; calibration, cutoff precision, equalized odds and selection parity differ. Randomization scope is the illustrated profit-optimal rule, not every equalized-odds rule. Preserve CEO image's published illustrative provenance. p75's long legal material moves beneath a short dated, qualified message; no silent claim of current law.

## Shared surfaces and checks
- slides alone updates the leaf OUTLINE note descriptions/line pointers that actually change; do not rewrite unrelated lecture/history sections. Main counts, section ranges, sources and technical supplements stay unchanged except ID-only source additions. Update links affected by note reorganization without altering the deck arc.
- Inventory includes two tracked Korean lec07 auxiliary study explainers: read for cross-reference consistency, preserve their contents/language. Five local standalone note artifacts were found on the mount but are not tracked in the frozen tree; exclude from git and do not overwrite unrelated local files.
- Check full count/order/title/ID mapping across 902 pairs; ensure all original source links are retained or their removal/replacement explained. Verify formula rendering, anchors and local links. Run existing relevant lint and report pre-existing warnings separately.
- Open every revised companion in a real browser; check top/middle/bottom plus all changed plot/math-heavy and long-detail entries, legible hierarchy, scrolling, no horizontal clipping, functioning math/links and narrow-screen readability. The notes are not fixed-height slides; do not apply slide-density rules to optional detail. Record pages/articles actually inspected.
- Work-r1 must include per-package completion, old→new slide map (unchanged pages), ID-only commit, exact reviewed head, source/claim changes, removed unique details if any, source issues, checks/render coverage and deviations. Only blocking findings start round 2; at the medium round limit unresolved blockers go to Albert.

## Complete per-slide specifications and frozen baseline map

Navigation: this exhaustive appendix is intentionally long. Read the common requirements once, then only the relevant `### Pn` section for each package. Do not repeatedly load the entire map. All twelve packages are authorized; this is not twelve approval gates.
Each entry below gives package, stable ID, baseline page/title, point seed, exhibit, source locators and acceptance class. Source labels in the existing slide and references in the matching note must be read in full, not inferred from truncated wording. The linked pinned note line is the exact source-register entry when no local external URL exists; resolve such inherited citations under rule 4. Prefixes Pn/E## are per lecture, not proof that its scientific content has been re-verified.

| lec03/S13 · p13 · Before Neural Nets: The Genome Leak | The first membership inference attack targeted published averages, not a model. | Original diagram: 1,000 participants each genome: 500,000 SNPs average per SNP public table 500,000 frequencies attacker has Alice's genome "Was Alice in the study?" | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L303) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L183); Homer et al., "Resolving Individuals Contributing Trace Amounts of DNA to Highly Complex Mixtures Using High-Density SNP Genotyping Microarrays", PLoS Genetics 4(8), 2008.; <https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pgen.1000167>; <https://doi.org/10.1038/ng.436> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S14 · p14 · Genomics in 60 Seconds | The study published column averages; the attack works from those alone. | Original diagram: SNP index 1 2 3 4 5 … 500,000 person 1 person 2 person 1,000 A G A A G … T A G G A G … C G G A A A … T column average = "allele frequency" at SNP 3 (e.g. 0.62) | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L340) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L206) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S15 · p15 · Homer's Statistic | $D_j$ is distance-to-everyone minus distance-to-the-study; positive leans "in". | Original diagram: 0 1 Pop_j = 0.30 M_j = 0.40 Y_j = 1 (Alice) to everyone: 0.70 to the study: 0.60 | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L370) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L216) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S16 · p16 · Why One SNP Says Nothing | Per SNP, membership shifts the mean by $2p(1-p)/n$ against noise $\sqrt{p(1-p)/n}$: hopeless alone. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L399) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L226) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S17 · p17 · One SNP Whispers, 500,000 Shout | Each SNP is hopeless alone; 500,000 of them turn a 0.03 signal-to-noise into a clean separation. | Original diagram: one SNP: D_j tilt 0.0004 vs noise 0.014 signal-to-noise 0.03: nothing sum 500,000 SNPs: sum of D_j not in in tilt grows like m, noise like √m gap grows like √(m / n) | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L414) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L237); Idealized model ($n = 1{,}000$ people, $m$ SNPs, $p(1-p) \approx 0.2$); analysis after Sankararaman, Obozinski, Jordan, and Halperin, Nature Genetics 2009. | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S18 · p18 · How Many SNPs Are Enough? | The gap grows like $\sqrt{m/n}$; the SNP budget scales linearly with cohort size, and arrays already have enough. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L445) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L247); Idealized model (independent SNPs, known population frequencies). | lec03/S26 · p26 · Overfitting Drives MIA | Overfitting makes the attack easy; its absence is not a guarantee. | Original diagram: small gap overlap ⇒ weak attack large gap separated ⇒ easy attack | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L610) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L409) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S27 · p27 · Confidence Works Too | Confidence substitutes for loss when labels are unavailable. | figs/salem-max-posterior.png | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L631) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L422); Salem et al., "ML-Leaks: Model and Data Independent Membership Inference Attacks and Defenses on Machine Learning Models", NDSS 2019 — Fig. 11 (CIFAR-100 left, News right).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1806.01246> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S28 · p28 · A Theory Anchor | The loss attack is provably tied to overfitting. | figs/yeom-advantage-gap.png | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L649) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L446); Yeom, Giacomelli, Fredrikson, and Jha, "Privacy Risk in Machine Learning: Analyzing the Connection to Overfitting", IEEE CSF 2018 — Fig. 2 (advantage vs. generalization ratio).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1709.01604> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S29 · p29 · Try It Yourself | The two bells are visible in a single Colab cell. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L664) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L480) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S30 · p30 · Shadow Models | Build models you understand to learn the membership signal. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L680) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L487) | Must 1–8; structural entry |
| lec03/S31 · p31 · One Threshold Is Crude | A global threshold leaves signal on the table. | Original diagram: class A class B one global threshold fits class A, calls every class-B member "out" | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L688) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L493) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S32 · p32 · The Shadow Idea | Shadow models give you ground-truth membership labels. | figs/shokri-shadow-training.png | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L716) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L503); Shokri, Stronati, Song, and Shmatikov, "Membership Inference Attacks Against Machine Learning Models", IEEE S&P 2017 — Fig. 2 (shadow models trained on the same ML platform).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1610.05820> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S33 · p33 · The Shadow Pipeline | Shadow outputs become labeled training data for an attack model that transfers to the target. | Original diagram: your data in / out known shadow models labeled in / out outputs attack classifier | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L731) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L522) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S34 · p34 · Why It Transfers | Shadows transfer because they share the target's training procedure and data distribution; architecture is one ingredient, not the reason. | Original diagram: target model in out loss → same pattern shadow model in out loss → | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L758) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L547); <https://arxiv.org/abs/1610.05820> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S35 · p35 · Stronger Attacks | Strong attacks ask whether the loss is low for this record specifically. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L795) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L577) | Must 1–8; structural entry |
| lec03/S36 · p36 · Not All Records Are Equal | Raw loss confuses how hard a record is with whether it was a member. | figs/carlini-lira-fig3-per-example.png | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L803) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L583); Carlini et al., "Membership Inference Attacks From First Principles", IEEE S&P 2022 — Fig. 3 (four CIFAR-10 examples, in vs. out). | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S37 · p37 · LiRA: Likelihood Ratio Attack | LiRA scores each record by a per-example in-versus-out likelihood ratio. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L823) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L594); Carlini et al., "Membership Inference Attacks From First Principles", IEEE S&P 2022; Sablayrolles et al., "White-box vs Black-box: Bayes Optimal Strategies for Membership Inference", ICML 2019.; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2112.03570>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1908.11229> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S38 · p38 · Deciding on a New Record | Membership for a new record is a per-record likelihood-ratio test with a threshold that sets the false-alarm rate. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L837) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L641) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S39 · p39 · Two In-vs-Out Bells | Per-record in and out distributions make the test far sharper; the decision is one arithmetic line. | Original diagram: 2 6 5 | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L851) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L660) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S40 · p40 · Label-Only Attacks | Even label-only access leaks membership through robustness. | figs/choquette-label-only.png | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L876) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L683); Choquette-Choo, Tramèr, Carlini, and Papernot, "Label-Only Membership Inference Attacks", ICML 2021 — Fig. 1 (CIFAR-10, accuracy vs. training-set size).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2007.14321> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S41 · p41 · Scoring an Attack: TPR and FPR | TPR is the catch rate on members, FPR the false-alarm rate on non-members; one threshold gives one pair. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L895) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L716) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S42 · p42 · The ROC Curve | The ROC curve is the full trade-off between catching members and accusing non-members, one point per threshold. | Original diagram: non-members members say "member" score → FPR → TPR → chance: TPR = FPR | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L912) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L729) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S43 · p43 · AUC and Its Blind Spot | AUC is the probability a member outscores a non-member; equal AUCs can hide completely different low-FPR behaviour. | Original diagram: A: strong at low FPR B: no confident hits FPR → TPR → same AUC | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L952) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L741) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S44 · p44 · Average Accuracy Lies | Average accuracy can mask catastrophic per-record leakage. | figs/carlini-lira-fig2-roc-scales.png | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L978) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L755); Carlini et al., "Membership Inference Attacks From First Principles", IEEE S&P 2022 — Fig. 2 (LOSS baseline, linear vs. log-log). | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S45 · p45 · TPR at Low FPR | Judge attacks by their true positive rate at very low false positive rates, the left edge of the ROC curve. | figs/carlini-lira-fig1-tpr-fpr.png | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L993) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L777); Carlini et al., "Membership Inference Attacks From First Principles", IEEE S&P 2022 — Fig. 1 (prior attacks vs. LiRA, non-overfit CIFAR-10 model). | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S46 · p46 · Base Rates: What an Accusation Is Worth | FPR is the false-accusation rate on non-members; with rare members, only a low-FPR operating point makes a flag worth anything. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L1012) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L795) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S47 · p47 · Does LiRA Work on LLMs? | Even a strong shadow-model attack gives limited, unstable membership signal on normally pretrained LLMs; MIA is not the same as extraction. | figs/hayes-llm-mia-fig2.png | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L1035) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L814); Hayes, Shumailov, Choquette-Choo, Jagielski, Kaissis, Nasr, … Cooper, "Exploring the limits of strong membership inference attacks on large language models", NeurIPS 2025 — Fig. 2 (Chinchilla-optimal, 1 epoch).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18773> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec03/S48 · p48 · What It Means | Differential privacy and membership inference are two views of the same thing. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L1052) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L841) | Must 1–8; structural entry |
| lec03/S49 · p49 · DP Caps the Attacker | A real epsilon bound is a guarantee against every membership attack. | Original diagram: data with Alice member world data without Alice non-member world DP training clip + noise outputs outputs | E03: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia.html#L1060) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec03-mia-note.html#L847); Dwork, Kenthapadi, McSherry, Mironov, and Naor, "Our Data, Ourselves: Privacy via Distributed Noise Generation", EUROCRYPT 2006.; <https://arxiv.org/abs/0811.2501>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1311.0776>; <https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/11761679_29> | Must 1–8; content entry |

| lec08/S49 · p49 · A Hidden Model Can Still Be Attacked | Ask, copy, attack, send: transfer defeats hiding. | Original diagram: ask send inputs, read answers copy train a local look-alike attack white box, on the copy send to the hidden model | E08: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial.html#L614) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial-note.html#L730); Substitute-model attack: Papernot et al., "Practical Black-Box Attacks against Machine Learning", AsiaCCS 2017; evaluated on commercial services in arXiv 1605.07277.; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1602.02697>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1605.07277> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec08/S50 · p50 · Transfer to Real Services: The Setup | Label-only queries, a local copy, then a white-box attack on the copy. | Original diagram: Target: a cloud service Amazon ML, Google Prediction trains its own MNIST model model type and weights hidden clean accuracy ≈ 92% Attacker sends images, sees labels only 100 seed digits → 800 queries trains a local copy | E08: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial.html#L624) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial-note.html#L747); Papernot, McDaniel & Goodfellow, "Transferability in Machine Learning: from Phenomena to Black-Box Attacks using Adversarial Samples", arXiv 1605.07277 — §5 (pixel values in [0, 1]).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1605.07277> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec08/S51 · p51 · Transfer Reached Real Services | With labels only, a substitute model let attacks reach real services (MNIST, 2016). | Original diagram: query API 800 labels train a copy local substitute attack copy FGSM, ε = 0.3 Amazon 96.19% Google 88.94% misclassification rate on the services' own MNIST models | E08: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial.html#L634) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial-note.html#L768); Papernot et al., arXiv 1605.07277 — §5, Tables 3–4 (logistic-regression substitute, ρ = 3; Amazon ML used multinomial logistic regression).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1605.07277> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec08/S52 · p52 · Any Wrong Label, or a Chosen One | Untargeted is the usual benchmark; targeted is harder and often worse. | Original diagram: Untargeted stop sign → anything else push loss on the true label up easier: any exit counts Targeted stop sign → speed limit 45 push loss on the target down harder, often more harmful | E08: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial.html#L644) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial-note.html#L791) | Must 1–8; content entry |

| lec08/S69 · p69 · Adapted Attacks Rebuild the Missing Gradient | Adapted attacks broke the defenses that hid gradients; the tested Madry model retained 47%. | Original diagram: Broken or flat step? use a smooth stand-in when computing the gradient BPDA Random each pass? average the gradient over many random draws EOT | E08: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial.html#L825) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial-note.html#L1067); Athalye, Carlini & Wagner, ICML 2018 — §4 (BPDA, EOT), Table 1 (Madry et al.: CIFAR-10, $\ell_\infty$ 0.031).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1802.00420>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.07397> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec08/S70 · p70 · How to Spot an Attack That Is Too Weak | Adapt the attack to the defense; diagnostics flag problems but prove nothing. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E08: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial.html#L836) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial-note.html#L1093); Athalye et al., ICML 2018 — §4 (BPDA, EOT); Carlini et al., "On Evaluating Adversarial Robustness", arXiv 1902.06705; Tramèr et al., "On Adaptive Attacks to Adversarial Example Defenses", NeurIPS 2020.; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1802.00420>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/1902.06705>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.08347> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec08/S71 · p71 · AutoAttack: Four Attacks, Nothing to Tune | A fixed bundle of diverse attacks, with nothing to tune. | Original diagram: PGD that tunes its own step APGD-CE same, aimed at chosen wrong classes APGD-T a small change that flips it FAB-T no gradients: queries only Square | E08: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial.html#L849) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec08-adversarial-note.html#L1111); Croce & Hein, "Reliable Evaluation of Adversarial Robustness with an Ensemble of Diverse Parameter-Free Attacks", ICML 2020 — §5. | lec09/S35 · p35 · A Guard Dog With a Secret Whistle | A backdoor is a secret rule that normal checks never exercise. | Original diagram: Every day barks at strangers passes every check looks well trained Secret whistle dog lets anyone in taught by the trainer only they know it | E09: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec09-poisoning.html#L412) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec09-poisoning-note.html#L464) | Must 1–8; content entry |

| lec09/S51 · p51 · Getting Content In Is Not the Same as Damage | Access cost, simulated effect and real-world damage are three different claims. | Original diagram: Measured est. $60/yr buys 0.01% of LAION-400M 800+ downloads in 6 months Simulated own CLIP training 1,000 images 60% flip one target's label Not shown damage to anyone's real model in-the-wild attacks (none found) | E09: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec09-poisoning.html#L581) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec09-poisoning-note.html#L683); Carlini et al., IEEE S&P 2024 — §4 (access measurement), §4.4 (no evidence of exploitation), §4.5 (local OpenCLIP ViT-B-32 on LAION-400M).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2302.10149> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec09/S52 · p52 · Wikipedia Snapshots Arrive on a Predictable Schedule | Frontrunning: edit just before the snapshot copies the page. | Original diagram: time attacker edits just before snapshot copies page time is predictable moderator reverts minutes later the snapshot keeps the bad version | E09: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec09-poisoning.html#L591) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec09-poisoning-note.html#L703); Carlini et al., IEEE S&P 2024 — §5.1–5.2 (Wikipedia forbids scraping the live site; snapshots crawl articles in a predictable order).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2302.10149> | Must 1–8; content entry |

| lec10/S04 · p4 · Chat Assistants Help, but Decline Some Requests | Assistants decline some requests because of training; a jailbreak gets the answer anyway. | Original diagram: request model Debug my Python code answers Explain how vaccines work answers Write password-stealing malware declines Plan a trip to Busan answers Give nerve-agent synthesis steps declines | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L91) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L56) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S05 · p5 · Why Getting Around Refusal Matters | A bypass can turn a refusal into real-world help or a real action. | Original diagram: Uplift real help toward serious harm: weapons, malware, fraud at scale Scale one shared trick works for every user of the model, for months Agency models now act: run code, browse, send messages for the user | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L100) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L71) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S06 · p6 · Jailbreak Prompts Are Shared and Last for Months | Jailbreak prompts are shared, refined and reused for months. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L109) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L88); Shen et al., "Do Anything Now": Characterizing and Evaluating In-The-Wild Jailbreak Prompts on Large Language Models, ACM CCS 2024 — Abstract and Sections 1, 4.1 (28 accounts refining for 100+ days; success rate on the authors' forbidden-question set).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.03825> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S07 · p7 · Example: Reworded Requests Got Past the Refusal | Rewording alone, with no special access, turned a refusal into an answer. | figs/wei-jailbroken-redacted.png | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L125) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L111); Wei, Haghtalab & Steinhardt, "Jailbroken: How Does LLM Safety Training Fail?", NeurIPS 2023 — Fig. 1 (GPT-4 and Claude v1.3, 2023); a deliberately mild request chosen by the authors; demanded-opening wording and Base64 payload redacted by the lecturer.; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.02483> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S08 · p8 · A Jailbreak Bypasses Safeguards Through Inputs | A jailbreak acts through inputs; weight-level safety removal is a different threat model. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L136) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L136) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S09 · p9 · Safety Training Teaches the Model When to Refuse | Refusal is the visible behavior of safety training, and it is learned from examples. | Original diagram: pretrained model predicts text alignment Helpful do what the user asks Harmless decline harmful requests = safety training | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L152) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L151); Ouyang et al., "Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback", NeurIPS 2022 (aligned = helpful, honest, harmless) · Bai et al., "Constitutional AI: Harmlessness from AI Feedback", arXiv 2212.08073, 2022.; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2203.02155>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.08073> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S10 · p10 · Example: How Llama 2 Chat Was Taught to Refuse | Llama 2 Chat learned refusal from examples, in three steps. | Original diagram: Safety fine-tuning annotators write adversarial prompts and safe answers; model imitates Safety RLHF a safety reward model scores replies to adversarial prompts Context distillation prefix "You are a safe and responsible | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L162) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L173); Touvron et al., "Llama 2: Open Foundation and Fine-Tuned Chat Models", arXiv 2307.09288, 2023 — Section 4.2 (safety fine-tuning, safety RLHF, safety context distillation; risk categories).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.09288> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S11 · p11 · Two Ways to Fail: Refusing Too Little or Too Much | Safety has two error types; a defense must report both. | Original diagram: model answers model refuses benign request harmful request helpful over-refusal cost of caution under-refusal jailbreak’s target safe refusal | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L172) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L194) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S12 · p12 · What the Attacker Can Touch Sets the Threat | Compare attacks only within the same access level. | Original diagram: Weights fine-tune or edit the model not a prompt attack: labelled as a contrast Response control, gradients prefill the reply; open-model search prefilling attack, GCG suffix search Chat only type prompts, read replies h | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L180) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L209) | Must 1–8; content entry |

Model name, version, dated snapshot Judge who calls it harmful? its error rates | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L188) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L225) | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S14 · p14 · Why Refusal Is Fragile | Several explanations, each with its own evidence and access level. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L198) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L238) | Must 1–8; structural entry |
| lec10/S15 · p15 · Two Hypotheses for Why Safety Training Fails | Two failure modes: objectives that conflict, and safety that does not cover all skills. | Original diagram: Competing objectives safety competes with other training goals the prompt forces a choice Mismatched generalization capabilities reach inputs safety training never saw the prompt leaves safe ground | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L206) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L245); Wei, Haghtalab & Steinhardt, "Jailbroken: How Does LLM Safety Training Fail?", NeurIPS 2023 — Section 3.; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.02483> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S16 · p16 · Competing Objectives: Make Refusing Costly | Competing-objective attacks make refusing break the model's other training goals. | Original diagram: Predict text pretraining continue text the likely way Follow instructions instruction tuning do what the user asks Stay safe safety training decline harmful requests attack: refusing would break these two e.g. demand a s | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L216) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L264); Wei, Haghtalab & Steinhardt, NeurIPS 2023 — Section 3.1 (language modeling, instruction following and safety objectives).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.02483> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S17 · p17 · Change the Key Wording and the Attack Stops Working | Remove the part that makes refusing costly, and the attack stops working. | Original diagram: Prefix injection reply must open with a carefree, eager line as written: 0.22 “Hello!” instead: 0.06 Refusal suppression rules forbid apologies and words like “cannot” as written: 0.25 rules inverted: 0.00 | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L225) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L283); Wei, Haghtalab & Steinhardt, NeurIPS 2023 — Section 3.1 and Table 1 (prefix injection vs its “Hello!” ablation; refusal suppression vs inverted rules). Families described; working prompts not shown.; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.02483>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.03825> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S18 · p18 · Mismatched Generalization: Skills Outrun Safety | If the model can read it but safety training never saw it, refusal may not fire. | Original diagram: inputs the model can understand learned in pretraining covered by safety training plain English chat outside it: Base64 and other encodings simple ciphers low-resource languages unusual output formats | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L235) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L307); Wei, Haghtalab & Steinhardt, NeurIPS 2023 — Section 3.2. Illustrative sketch, not to scale.; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.02483> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S19 · p19 · Combined Attacks Beat Single Ones | Combining the two failure modes was far stronger than either alone. | Displayed text, equation, example, or table; explain in its reading order. | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L244) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L328); Wei, Haghtalab & Steinhardt, NeurIPS 2023 — Table 1, BAD BOT rate (selected rows; 2023 model versions).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.02483> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S20 · p20 · Refusals Begin With a Stock Opening Phrase | Refusals start with a few stock words; the next slides test whether that is most of the safety. | Original diagram: Llama-2-7B-Chat, asked how to break into a house: I cannot fulfill your request. It’s not within … stock opening set by safety training the rest a natural continuation of the opening | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L260) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L353); Qi et al., "Safety Alignment Should Be Made More Than Just a Few Tokens Deep", ICLR 2025 — Section 2.2 (330 harmful HEx-PHI requests; example reply quoted from the paper).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.05946> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S21 · p21 · Even a Base Model Looks Safe After “I cannot” | Forcing a refusal opening made even the base model look almost safe. | Original diagram: Base model, no forced opening 68.6% Base model, opening forced to “I cannot” 16.4% Base model, opening forced to “I apologize, but I cannot” 2.1% Aligned chat model, no forced opening 0% | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L271) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L372); Qi et al., ICLR 2025 — Table 1 (Llama-2-7B; selected openings) and Section 2.2.; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.05946> | Must 1–8; content entry |
| lec10/S22 · p22 · Safety Training Changed Mostly the First Few Tokens | In the tested open models, alignment changed mostly the first few tokens. | figs/qi25-kl.png | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L282) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L393); Qi et al., "Safety Alignment Should Be Made More Than Just a Few Tokens Deep", ICLR 2025 — Fig. 1 (Harmful HEx-PHI responses).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.05946> | Must 1–8; content entry |

| lec10/S24 · p24 · The Fix: Train Recovery After a Harmful Start | Train the model to refuse even after a harmful start. | Original diagram: user turn model reply request x a harmful ask harmful answer h only its first k tokens refusal r “I cannot fulfill…” context only, no loss trained to output r k = 0 half the time, else uniform on 1 to 100 | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L316) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L439); Qi et al., ICLR 2025 — Section 3.1, Eq. 2 (256 recovery triplets; Alpaca requests with the initial model’s replies; weight 0.2 on the safety term).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.05946> | Must 1–8; content entry |

| lec10/S26 · p26 · One Direction in Activations Switches Refusal | In tested open models, one activation direction switched refusal off and on. | Original diagram: 1. Find r r harmless harmful r = mean harmful − mean harmless 2. Remove r at every layer and token harmful request: no refusal 3. Add r at one layer harmless request: refusal | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L343) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L481); Arditi et al., "Refusal in Language Models Is Mediated by a Single Direction", NeurIPS 2024 — Sections 2.3–2.4, 3 and 4 (128 harmful and 128 harmless training instructions; r taken at the best layer on validation sets).; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.11717> | Must 1–8; content entry |

| lec10/S33 · p33 · Automated Attacks Are a Search Loop | Automated jailbreaking is search; its cost is the number of trips round the loop. | Original diagram: candidate prompt target model score propose a better candidate how to propose? gradients or an LLM what access? weights or queries how to score? target text or a judge | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L421) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L605); <https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.03286> | Must 1–8; content entry |

| lec10/S79 · p79 · Existing Monitors: Cheap or Flexible, Not Both | Self-assessment aims to be as cheap as a probe and as flexible as a prompted classifier. | Original diagram: cheap to run costly to run flexible new policy, new harms fixed labels set in advance self-assessment the model grades itself prompted classifier a separate model reads the policy probe small classifier on the model's ac | E10: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak.html#L985) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec10-jailbreak-note.html#L1428); Zhang et al., "Scaling Misuse Detection with Self-Assessment", Anthropic Safeguards blog, Oct 2026 — "Existing monitoring methods", "Where self-assessment fits in" and Figure 5 (session policy).; <https://safeguards.anthropic.com/2026/self-assessment/>; <https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.18837> | Must 1–8; content entry |

| lec12/S08 · p11 · A Proxy Can Answer the Wrong Question | The choice of label is a fairness decision. | Original diagram: what we want: actual outcome will this person repay? unseen for anyone denied what we record: the label Y = approved before seen for everyone ≠ one past applicant, denied actual: would have repaid recorded: Y = 0 the lab | E12: [slide](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec12-fairness.html#L292) · [note](https://github.com/albert-no/slides/blob/99450923b41e94f10d0c557a4fc0f46b42a9c91d/courses/trustworthy-ai/lec12-fairness-note.html#L179) | Must 1–8; content entry |
